1	% Section 5 — SciERC Results (Draft)
2	% Data source: Phase 2 SciERC registry experiments
3	% exp_ids: phase2_scierc_unconstrained, phase2_scierc_grammar_only,
4	%          phase2_scierc_semantic_only, phase2_scierc_full_s1, phase2_scierc_full_s2
5	% All numbers verified against registry 2026-05-14.
6	
7	\subsection{Results on SciERC}
8	\label{sec:scierc-results}
9	
10	Table~\ref{tab:scierc-ablation} presents the five-way ablation on SciERC,
11	evaluating each constraint layer independently and in combination.
12	All experiments use Qwen3-8B in a zero-shot setting on 551 test documents
13	with identical prompts and decoding parameters;
14	only the active constraint layers differ.
15	
16	% ──────────────────────────────────────────
17	%  Table: SciERC five-way ablation
18	% ──────────────────────────────────────────
19	\begin{table}[t]
20	\centering
21	\small
22	\setlength{\tabcolsep}{3.6pt}
23	\begin{tabular}{@{}l rrrrr rr@{}}

27	\cmidrule(lr){2-6} \cmidrule(l){7-8}
28	\textbf{Mode}
29	  & DR\,$\downarrow$ & $\Delta$DR
30	  & RV\,$\downarrow$ & ET\,$\downarrow$ & CF
31	  & Rel.\,$\uparrow$ & Ent.\,$\uparrow$ \\
32	\midrule
33	Unconstrained
34	  & 15.37 & ---
35	  & 1.39  & 0.40
36	  & 0.08
37	  & 8.23  & 31.01 \\
38	Grammar
39	  & 14.84 & $\downarrow$3\%
40	  & \textbf{0} & \textbf{0}
41	  & 0.08
42	  & 8.23  & 30.98 \\
43	Semantic
44	  & 11.65 & $\downarrow$24\%
45	  & 1.51  & 0.40
46	  & 2.71
47	  & 8.15  & 31.01 \\
48	Full (S1)
49	  & \textbf{0.93} & $\downarrow$\textbf{94\%}
50	  & \textbf{0} & \textbf{0}
51	  & 9.90
52	  & \textbf{8.36} & 30.98 \\
53	Full (S2)
54	  & 0.97  & $\downarrow$94\%
55	  & \textbf{0} & \textbf{0}
56	  & 8.03
57	  & 8.03  & \textbf{31.90} \\
58	\bottomrule
59	\end{tabular}
60	\caption{Five-way ablation on SciERC (551 documents, Qwen3-8B, zero-shot).
61	DR: domain/range violation rate;
62	RV: relation vocabulary violation rate;
63	ET: entity type violation rate;
64	CF: cross-field consistency violation rate.
65	$\Delta$DR: relative DR reduction vs.\ the unconstrained baseline.
66	$\downarrow$\,/\,$\uparrow$: lower\,/\,higher is better.
67	Best per column in \textbf{bold} (CF excluded---see text).
68	F1 scores are multiplied by 100.}
69	\label{tab:scierc-ablation}
70	\end{table}
71	
72	% ──────────────────────────────────────────
73	%  (a) Core result
74	% ──────────────────────────────────────────
75	\paragraph{Core result: near-complete DR elimination at zero F1 cost.}
76	The full two-layer constraint configuration (S1) reduces domain/range
77	violations from 15.37\% to 0.93\%, a 94\% relative reduction,
78	% phase2_scierc_unconstrained: DR=0.1537; phase2_scierc_full_s1: DR=0.0093
79	while relation F1 moves from 8.23 to 8.36 (+0.13\,pp).
80	% phase2_scierc_unconstrained: R-F1=0.0823; phase2_scierc_full_s1: R-F1=0.0836
81	This difference is not statistically significant and is best
82	characterized as F1-neutral: constraint enforcement neither improves
83	nor degrades extraction quality.
84	At the per-relation level, violations drop from 42.42\% to 2.82\% on
85	\textsc{Compare}, from 33.54\% to 1.21\% on \textsc{Feature-of}, and
86	reach 0\% on four of seven relation types
87	(\textsc{Conjunction}, \textsc{Hyponym-of}, \textsc{Part-of},
88	\textsc{Used-for}).
89	% phase2_scierc_full_s1 per_relation_dr: Compare=0.0282,
90	%   Feature-of=0.0121, Hyponym-of=0, Part-of=0, Used-for=0, Conjunction=0
91	However, full constraint enforcement elevates cross-field consistency
92	violations from 0.08\% to 9.90\%;
93	% phase2_scierc_unconstrained: CF=0.0008; phase2_scierc_full_s1: CF=0.099
94	we analyze this trade-off in Section~\ref{sec:discussion}.
95	
96	% ──────────────────────────────────────────
97	%  (b) Grammar layer
98	% ──────────────────────────────────────────
99	\paragraph{Grammar layer: cost-free structural guarantees.}
100	Grammar-only constraints completely eliminate both relation vocabulary
101	violations (1.39\%\,$\to$\,0\%) and entity type violations
102	(0.40\%\,$\to$\,0\%), with no measurable impact on F1
103	(R-F1 8.23, E-F1 30.98).
104	% phase2_scierc_grammar_only: RV=0, ET=0, R-F1=0.0823, E-F1=0.3098
105	Domain/range violations remain effectively unchanged
106	(15.37\%\,$\to$\,14.84\%, $-$0.53\,pp), confirming that grammar
107	constraints enforce output structure---valid JSON, correct relation
108	vocabulary, valid entity types---but do not address cross-field
109	semantic constraints.
110	% phase2_scierc_grammar_only: DR=0.1484
111	The grammar layer thus provides two categories of violation elimination
112	at zero cost, establishing the structural preconditions on which the
113	semantic layer depends.
114	
115	% ──────────────────────────────────────────
116	%  (c) Enabling dependency
117	% ──────────────────────────────────────────
118	\paragraph{Enabling dependency: grammar enables semantic enforcement.}
119	Applying semantic constraints alone reduces DR by only 24\%
120	(15.37\%\,$\to$\,11.65\%) and, critically, cannot eliminate
121	relation vocabulary violations (RV\,=\,1.51\%) or entity type
122	violations (ET\,=\,0.40\%).
123	% phase2_scierc_semantic_only: DR=0.1165, RV=0.0151, ET=0.004
124	When the grammar layer is added (Full S1), DR drops a further 92\%
125	relative to the semantic-only configuration
126	(11.65\%\,$\to$\,0.93\%).
127	% phase2_scierc_full_s1: DR=0.0093; delta=(11.65-0.93)/11.65=0.920
128	This demonstrates an \emph{enabling dependency} rather than mere
129	additivity: without grammar-enforced vocabulary constraints, the
130	semantic layer encounters out-of-vocabulary relation types for which
131	no domain/range rules exist, rendering its type checking ineffective.
132	The two layers are therefore complementary with a prerequisite
133	structure---grammar must be active for semantic constraints to reach
134	their full enforcement capacity.
135	
136	% ──────────────────────────────────────────
137	%  (d) S1 vs S2
138	% ──────────────────────────────────────────
139	\paragraph{S1 vs.\ S2: single-pass suffices.}
140	The two-pass strategy (S2) achieves comparable DR
141	(0.97\% vs.\ 0.93\%) but lower relation F1
142	(8.03 vs.\ 8.36, $-$0.33\,pp) and generates fewer relation triples
143	(1{,}644 vs.\ 1{,}729).
144	% phase2_scierc_full_s2: DR=0.0097, R-F1=0.0803, triples=1644
145	% phase2_scierc_full_s1: DR=0.0093, R-F1=0.0836, triples=1729
146	S2 shows a modestly lower CF rate (8.03\% vs.\ 9.90\%), but both
147	configurations exhibit substantially elevated CF relative to the
148	unconstrained baseline (0.08\%), indicating that two-pass decoding
149	does not resolve the cross-field consistency trade-off
150	(Section~\ref{sec:discussion}).
151	% phase2_scierc_full_s2: CF=0.0803; phase2_scierc_full_s1: CF=0.099
152	The two-pass separation offers no clear advantage over single-pass
153	enforcement; we adopt S1 as the primary configuration for its
154	simplicity and marginally stronger extraction performance.
155

1	% Section 5 — DocRED Results (Draft)
2	% Data source: Phase 2 DocRED registry experiments (checker_version: fixed_20260514)
3	% exp_ids: phase2_docred_unconstrained, phase2_docred_grammar_only,
4	%          phase2_docred_semantic_only, phase2_docred_full_s1
5	% All numbers verified against registry 2026-05-14.
6	% F1 computed via all_set method with name→Wikidata PID normalization.
7	
8	\subsection{Cross-Dataset Validation on DocRED}
9	\label{sec:docred-results}
10	
11	Table~\ref{tab:docred-ablation} presents the four-way ablation on DocRED,
12	a document-level relation extraction benchmark with 96 Wikidata relation
13	types---14$\times$ the ontology size of SciERC
14	(Section~\ref{sec:scierc-results}).
15	All experiments use Qwen3-8B in a zero-shot setting on 998 test documents
16	with identical prompts and decoding parameters.
17	Relation F1 is computed via name-to-Wikidata-PID normalization:
18	each predicted relation name is mapped to its canonical PID before
19	matching against gold annotations, and predictions that cannot be mapped
20	to any valid PID are counted as false positives.
21	
22	% ──────────────────────────────────────────
23	%  Table: DocRED four-way ablation
24	% ──────────────────────────────────────────
25	\begin{table}[t]
26	\centering
27	\small
28	\setlength{\tabcolsep}{3.6pt}
29	\begin{tabular}{@{}l rrrrr rr@{}}
30	\toprule
31	& \multicolumn{5}{c}{\textbf{Violation Rates (\%)}}
32	& \multicolumn{2}{c}{\textbf{F1}} \\
33	\cmidrule(lr){2-6} \cmidrule(l){7-8}
34	\textbf{Mode}
35	  & DR\,$\downarrow$ & $\Delta$DR
36	  & RV\,$\downarrow$ & ET\,$\downarrow$ & CF
37	  & Rel.\,$\uparrow$ & Ent.\,$\uparrow$ \\
38	\midrule
39	Unconstrained
40	  & 18.46 & ---
41	  & 29.66 & 8.18
42	  & 0.10
43	  & 7.74  & 58.15 \\
44	Grammar
45	  & 26.17 & $\uparrow$42\%
46	  & \textbf{0.03} & \textbf{0}
47	  & 0.07
48	  & \textbf{8.31}  & --- \\
49	Semantic
50	  & 13.84 & $\downarrow$25\%
51	  & 29.87 & 10.44
52	  & 1.05
53	  & 7.70  & 57.81 \\
54	Full (S1)
55	  & \textbf{10.19} & $\downarrow$\textbf{45\%}
56	  & \textbf{0.03} & \textbf{0}
57	  & 2.57
58	  & 8.23  & \textbf{59.37} \\
59	\bottomrule
60	\end{tabular}
61	\caption{Four-way ablation on DocRED (998 documents, Qwen3-8B, zero-shot).
62	DR: domain/range violation rate;
63	RV: relation vocabulary violation rate;
64	ET: entity type violation rate;
65	CF: cross-field consistency violation rate.
66	$\Delta$DR: relative change vs.\ the unconstrained baseline
67	($\uparrow$: increase; $\downarrow$: decrease).
68	Best per column in \textbf{bold} (CF excluded---see text).
69	F1 scores are multiplied by 100.
70	Grammar-only entity F1 is unavailable (---) because the grammar
71	constraint restricts relation vocabulary but does not modify entity
72	extraction, preventing meaningful entity-level evaluation in isolation.}
73	\label{tab:docred-ablation}
74	\end{table}
75	
76	% ──────────────────────────────────────────
77	%  (a) Core result
78	% ──────────────────────────────────────────
79	\paragraph{Core result: DR halved at zero F1 cost.}
80	The full two-layer configuration reduces domain/range violations from
81	18.46\% to 10.19\%, a 45\% relative reduction, while simultaneously
82	eliminating entity type violations (8.18\%\,$\to$\,0\%) and
83	near-eliminating relation vocabulary violations
84	(29.66\%\,$\to$\,0.03\%).
85	% phase2_docred_unconstrained: DR=0.1846, ET=0.0818, RV=0.2966
86	% phase2_docred_full_s1: DR=0.1019, ET=0, RV=0.0003
87	Relation F1 moves from 7.74 to 8.23 (+0.49\,pp) and entity F1 from
88	58.15 to 59.37 (+1.22\,pp);
89	% phase2_docred_unconstrained: R-F1=0.0774, E-F1=0.5815
90	% phase2_docred_full_s1: R-F1=0.0823, E-F1=0.5937
91	as with SciERC, these differences are within noise for single-seed
92	evaluation and are best characterized as F1-neutral.
93	Cross-field consistency violations increase modestly from 0.10\% to
94	2.57\%, substantially lower than the 9.90\% observed on SciERC;
95	% phase2_docred_unconstrained: CF=0.0010; phase2_docred_full_s1: CF=0.0257
96	we discuss this trade-off in Section~\ref{sec:discussion}.
97	
98	% ──────────────────────────────────────────
99	%  (b) Grammar layer
100	% ──────────────────────────────────────────
101	\paragraph{Grammar layer: structural guarantees generalize, but surface latent violations.}
102	Grammar-only constraints generalize to DocRED's 96-relation ontology,
103	eliminating entity type violations (8.18\%\,$\to$\,0\%) and
104	near-eliminating relation vocabulary violations
105	(29.66\%\,$\to$\,0.03\%).
106	% phase2_docred_grammar_only: ET=0, RV=0.0003
107	However, domain/range violations \emph{increase} from 18.46\% to
108	26.17\% (+42\% relative).
109	% phase2_docred_grammar_only: DR=0.2617
110	This counter-intuitive result has a structural explanation.
111	In the unconstrained baseline, 29.66\% of triples contain
112	out-of-vocabulary relation names and are classified as RV violations;
113	because no schema entry exists for an invalid relation name,
114	these triples \emph{cannot} be evaluated against domain/range rules
115	and are therefore absent from the DR count.
116	When grammar constraints force all relation names into the valid
117	vocabulary, these formerly RV-classified triples receive valid
118	relation names---but the associated entity types frequently violate
119	the domain/range rules of the assigned relation, surfacing as new
120	DR violations.
121	The grammar layer thus \emph{reveals} latent type incompatibilities
122	rather than creating them, directly motivating the semantic layer:
123	without domain/range enforcement, grammar alone makes the type
124	violation landscape worse, not better.
125	
126	% ──────────────────────────────────────────
127	%  (c) Enabling dependency
128	% ──────────────────────────────────────────
129	\paragraph{Enabling dependency confirmed across datasets.}
130	Semantic constraints alone reduce DR by 25\%
131	(18.46\%\,$\to$\,13.84\%) but cannot eliminate relation vocabulary
132	violations (RV\,=\,29.87\%) or entity type violations
133	(ET\,=\,10.44\%).
134	% phase2_docred_semantic_only: DR=0.1384, RV=0.2987, ET=0.1044
135	The elevated ET under semantic-only decoding (10.44\% vs.\ 8.18\%
136	unconstrained) reflects a genuine trajectory perturbation:
137	constrained logit masking shifts the generation distribution,
138	causing the model to produce more non-standard entity types
139	(e.g., \textsc{Work}, \textsc{Event}, \textsc{Ethnic\_Group})
140	that fall outside DocRED's canonical six-type vocabulary.
141	% Top non-standard types: WORK (823), EVENT (743), ETHNIC_GROUP (137)
142	We discuss this side effect in the limitations.
143	Combining both layers (Full S1) reduces DR a further 26\% relative to
144	the semantic-only configuration (13.84\%\,$\to$\,10.19\%),
145	% phase2_docred_full_s1: DR=0.1019; delta=(13.84-10.19)/13.84=0.264
146	replicating the enabling dependency observed on SciERC:
147	without grammar-enforced vocabulary constraints, the semantic layer
148	encounters out-of-vocabulary relation types for which no domain/range
149	rules exist, limiting its enforcement capacity.
150	The consistency of this pattern across two datasets with substantially
151	different ontology sizes (7 vs.\ 96 relation types) and violation
152	profiles (SciERC RV\,=\,1.39\% vs.\ DocRED RV\,=\,29.66\%) confirms
153	that the grammar$\to$semantic enabling dependency is a general
154	architectural property of the framework, not an artifact of a
155	particular schema.
156	
157	% ──────────────────────────────────────────
158	%  (d) Constraint tightness scaling
159	% ──────────────────────────────────────────
160	\paragraph{Constraint tightness governs enforcement ceiling.}
161	The residual DR on DocRED (10.19\%) substantially exceeds that of
162	SciERC (0.93\%, Section~\ref{sec:scierc-results}).
163	This gap is explained by per-relation \emph{constraint tightness}:
164	DocRED relations admit on average only 5.68 valid entity type pairs
165	(mean tightness\,=\,0.842, i.e., 84.2\% of entity type pairs violate
166	domain/range rules), compared to 23 valid pairs on SciERC
167	(tightness\,=\,0.333).
168	% DocRED: avg_valid_pairs=5.68, avg_tightness=0.842
169	% SciERC: avg_valid_pairs=23, avg_tightness=0.333
170	With tighter per-relation constraints, the semantic layer's logit mask
171	eliminates a larger fraction of candidate tokens at each decoding step,
172	increasing the probability that the intersection of grammar and
173	semantic masks yields a narrow or empty candidate set.
174	In the limiting case, the system falls back to grammar-only constraints,
175	preserving structural validity but forgoing domain/range enforcement
176	for that token position.
177	Conversely, SciERC's looser constraints leave more valid alternatives
178	for the model to redirect probability mass toward, enabling
179	near-complete DR elimination.
180	The DR reduction thus scales inversely with constraint tightness:
181	45\% on DocRED (tight) vs.\ 94\% on SciERC (loose).
182	% DocRED: DR_reduction=45%; SciERC: DR_reduction=94%
183	This represents, to our knowledge, the first empirical characterization
184	of how ontology constraint properties modulate decoding-time enforcement
185	effectiveness; we develop this analysis further in
186	Section~\ref{sec:discussion}.
187

counter-intuitive result has a structural explanation.\nIn the unconstrained baseline, 29.66\\% of triples contain\nout-of-vocabulary relation names and are classified as RV violations;\nbecause no schema entry exists for an invalid relation name,\nthese triples \\emph{cannot} be evaluated against domain/range rules\nand are therefore absent from the DR count.\nWhen grammar constraints force all relation names into the valid\nvocabulary, these formerly RV-classified triples receive valid\nrelation names---but the associated entity types frequently violate\nthe domain/range rules of the assigned relation, surfacing as new\nDR violations.\nThe grammar layer thus \\emph{reveals} latent type incompatibilities\nrather than creating them, directly motivating the semantic layer:\nwithout domain/range enforcement, grammar alone makes the type\nviolation landscape worse, not better.\n\n% ──────────────────────────────────────────\n%  (c) Enabling dependency\n% ──────────────────────────────────────────\n\\paragraph{Enabling dependency confirmed across datasets.}\nSemantic constraints alone reduce DR by 25\\%\n(18.46\\%\\,$\\to$\\,13.84\\%) but cannot eliminate relation vocabulary\nviolations (RV\\,=\\,29.87\\%) or entity type violations\n(ET\\,=\\,10.44\\%).\n% phase2_docred_semantic_only: DR=0.1384, RV=0.2987, ET=0.1044\nThe elevated ET under semantic-only decoding (10.44\\% vs.\\ 8.18\\%\nunconstrained) reflects a genuine trajectory perturbation:\nconstrained logit masking shifts the generation distribution,\ncausing the model to produce more non-standard entity types\n(e.g., \\textsc{Work}, \\textsc{Event}, \\textsc{Ethnic\\_Group})\nthat fall outside DocRED's canonical six-type vocabulary.\n% Top non-standard types: WORK (823), EVENT (743), ETHNIC_GROUP (137)\nWe discuss this side effect in the limitations.\nCombining both layers (Full S1) reduces DR a further 26\\% relative to\nthe semantic-only configuration (13.84\\%\\,$\\to$\\,10.19\\%),\n% phase2_docred_full_s1: DR=0.1019; delta=(13.84-10.19)/13.84=0.264\nreplicating the enabling dependency observed on SciERC:\nwithout grammar-enforced vocabulary constraints, the semantic layer\nencounters out-of-vocabulary relation types for which no domain/range\nrules exist, limiting its enforcement capacity.\nThe consistency of this pattern across two datasets with substantially\ndifferent ontology sizes (7 vs.\\ 96 relation types) and violation\nprofiles (SciERC RV\\,=\\,1.39\\% vs.\\ DocRED RV\\,=\\,29.66\\%) confirms\nthat the grammar$\\to$semantic enabling dependency is a general\narchitectural property of the framework, not an artifact of a\nparticular schema.\n\n% ──────────────────────────────────────────\n%  (d) Constraint tightness scaling\n% ──────────────────────────────────────────\n\\paragraph{Constraint tightness governs enforcement ceiling.}\nThe residual DR on DocRED (10.19\\%) substantially exceeds that of\nSciERC (0.93\\%, Section~\\ref{sec:scierc-results}).\nThis gap is explained by per-relation \\emph{constraint tightness}:\nDocRED relations admit on average only 5.68 valid entity type pairs\n(mean tightness\\,=\\,0.842, i.e., 84.2\\% of entity type pairs violate\ndomain/range rules), compared to 23 valid pairs on SciERC\n(tightness\\,=\\,0.333).\n% DocRED: avg_valid_pairs=5.68, avg_tightness=0.842\n% SciERC: avg_valid_pairs=23, avg_tightness=0.333\nWith tighter per-relation constraints, the semantic layer's logit mask\neliminates a larger fraction of candidate tokens at each decoding step,\nincreasing the probability that the intersection of grammar and\nsemantic masks yields a narrow or empty candidate set.\nIn the limiting case, the system falls back to grammar-only constraints,\npreserving structural validity but forgoing domain/range enforcement\nfor that token position.\nConversely, SciERC's looser constraints leave more valid alternatives\nfor the model to redirect probability mass toward, enabling\nnear-complete DR elimination.\nThe DR reduction thus scales inversely with constraint tightness:\n45\\% on DocRED (tight) vs.\\ 94\\% on SciERC (loose).\n% DocRED: DR_reduction=45%; SciERC: DR_reduction=94%\nThis represents, to our knowledge, the first empirical characterization\nof how ontology constraint properties modulate decoding-time enforcement\neffectiveness; we develop this analysis further in\nSection~\\ref{sec:discussion}.\n",

relation names into the valid\nvocabulary, these formerly RV-classified triples receive valid\nrelation names---but the associated entity types frequently violate\nthe domain/range rules of the assigned relation, surfacing as new\nDR violations.\nThe grammar layer thus \\emph{reveals} latent type incompatibilities\nrather than creating them, directly motivating the semantic layer:\nwithout domain/range enforcement, grammar alone makes the type\nviolation landscape worse, not better.\n\n% ──────────────────────────────────────────\n%  (c) Enabling dependency\n% ──────────────────────────────────────────\n\\paragraph{Enabling dependency confirmed across datasets.}\nSemantic constraints alone reduce DR by 25\\%\n(18.46\\%\\,$\\to$\\,13.84\\%) but cannot eliminate relation vocabulary\nviolations (RV\\,=\\,29.87\\%) or entity type violations\n(ET\\,=\\,10.44\\%).\n% phase2_docred_semantic_only: DR=0.1384, RV=0.2987, ET=0.1044\nThe elevated ET under semantic-only decoding (10.44\\% vs.\\ 8.18\\%\nunconstrained) reflects a genuine trajectory perturbation:\nconstrained logit masking shifts the generation distribution,\ncausing the model to produce more non-standard entity types\n(e.g., \\textsc{Work}, \\textsc{Event}, \\textsc{Ethnic\\_Group})\nthat fall outside DocRED's canonical six-type vocabulary.\n% Top non-standard types: WORK (823), EVENT (743), ETHNIC_GROUP (137)\nWe discuss this side effect in the limitations.\nCombining both layers (Full S1) reduces DR a further 26\\% relative to\nthe semantic-only configuration (13.84\\%\\,$\\to$\\,10.19\\%),\n% phase2_docred_full_s1: DR=0.1019; delta=(13.84-10.19)/13.84=0.264\nreplicating the enabling dependency observed on SciERC:\nwithout grammar-enforced vocabulary constraints, the semantic layer\nencounters out-of-vocabulary relation types for which no domain/range\nrules exist, limiting its enforcement capacity.\nThe consistency of this pattern across two datasets with substantially\ndifferent ontology sizes (7 vs.\\ 96 relation types) and violation\nprofiles (SciERC RV\\,=\\,1.39\\% vs.\\ DocRED RV\\,=\\,29.66\\%) confirms\nthat the grammar$\\to$semantic enabling dependency is a general\narchitectural property of the framework, not an artifact of a\nparticular schema.\n\n% ──────────────────────────────────────────\n%  (d) Constraint tightness scaling\n% ──────────────────────────────────────────\n\\paragraph{Constraint tightness governs enforcement ceiling.}\nThe residual DR on DocRED (10.19\\%) substantially exceeds that of\nSciERC (0.93\\%, Section~\\ref{sec:scierc-results}).\nThis gap is explained by per-relation \\emph{constraint tightness}:\nDocRED relations admit on average only 5.68 valid entity type pairs\n(mean tightness\\,=\\,0.842, i.e., 84.2\\% of entity type pairs violate\ndomain/range rules), compared to 23 valid pairs on SciERC\n(tightness\\,=\\,0.333).\n% DocRED: avg_valid_pairs=5.68, avg_tightness=0.842\n% SciERC: avg_valid_pairs=23, avg_tightness=0.333\nWith tighter per-relation constraints, the semantic layer's logit mask\neliminates a larger fraction of candidate tokens at each decoding step,\nincreasing the probability that the intersection of grammar and\nsemantic masks yields a narrow or empty candidate set.\nIn the limiting case, the system falls back to grammar-only constraints,\npreserving structural validity but forgoing domain/range enforcement\nfor that token position.\nConversely, SciERC's looser constraints leave more valid alternatives\nfor the model to redirect probability mass toward, enabling\nnear-complete DR elimination.\nThe DR reduction thus scales inversely with constraint tightness:\n45\\% on DocRED (tight) vs.\\ 94\\% on SciERC (loose).\n% DocRED: DR_reduction=45%; SciERC: DR_reduction=94%\nThis represents, to our knowledge, the first empirical characterization\nof how ontology constraint properties modulate decoding-time enforcement\neffectiveness; we develop this analysis further in\nSection~\\ref{sec:discussion}.\n",

relation names into the valid\nvocabulary, these formerly RV-classified triples receive valid\nrelation names---but the associated entity types frequently violate\nthe domain/range rules of the assigned relation, surfacing as new\nDR violations.\nThe grammar layer thus \\emph{reveals} latent type incompatibilities\nrather than creating them, directly motivating the semantic layer:\nwithout domain/range enforcement, grammar alone makes the type\nviolation landscape worse, not better.\n\n% ──────────────────────────────────────────\n%  (c) Enabling dependency\n% ──────────────────────────────────────────\n\\paragraph{Enabling dependency confirmed across datasets.}\nSemantic constraints alone reduce DR by 25\\%\n(18.46\\%\\,$\\to$\\,13.84\\%) but cannot eliminate relation vocabulary\nviolations (RV\\,=\\,29.87\\%) or entity type violations\n(ET\\,=\\,10.44\\%).\n% phase2_docred_semantic_only: DR=0.1384, RV=0.2987, ET=0.1044\nThe elevated ET under semantic-only decoding (10.44\\% vs.\\ 8.18\\%\nunconstrained) reflects a genuine trajectory perturbation:\nconstrained logit masking shifts the generation distribution,\ncausing the model to produce more non-standard entity types\n(e.g., \\textsc{Work}, \\textsc{Event}, \\textsc{Ethnic\\_Group})\nthat fall outside DocRED's canonical six-type vocabulary.\n% Top non-standard types: WORK (823), EVENT (743), ETHNIC_GROUP (137)\nWe discuss this side effect in the limitations.\nCombining both layers (Full S1) reduces DR a further 26\\% relative to\nthe semantic-only configuration (13.84\\%\\,$\\to$\\,10.19\\%),\n% phase2_docred_full_s1: DR=0.1019; delta=(13.84-10.19)/13.84=0.264\nreplicating the enabling dependency observed on SciERC:\nwithout grammar-enforced vocabulary constraints, the semantic layer\nencounters out-of-vocabulary relation types for which no domain/range\nrules exist, limiting its enforcement capacity.\nThe consistency of this pattern across two datasets with substantially\ndifferent ontology sizes (7 vs.\\ 96 relation types) and violation\nprofiles (SciERC RV\\,=\\,1.39\\% vs.\\ DocRED RV\\,=\\,29.66\\%) confirms\nthat the grammar$\\to$semantic enabling dependency is a general\narchitectural property of the framework, not an artifact of a\nparticular schema.\n\n% ──────────────────────────────────────────\n%  (d) Constraint tightness scaling\n% ──────────────────────────────────────────\n\\paragraph{Constraint tightness governs enforcement ceiling.}\nThe residual DR on DocRED (10.19\\%) substantially exceeds that of\nSciERC (0.93\\%, Section~\\ref{sec:scierc-results}).\nThis gap is explained by per-relation \\emph{constraint tightness}:\nDocRED relations admit on average only 5.68 valid entity type pairs\n(mean tightness\\,=\\,0.842, i.e., 84.2\\% of entity type pairs violate\ndomain/range rules), compared to 23 valid pairs on SciERC\n(tightness\\,=\\,0.333).\n% DocRED: avg_valid_pairs=5.68, avg_tightness=0.842\n% SciERC: avg_valid_pairs=23, avg_tightness=0.333\nWith tighter per-relation constraints, the semantic layer's logit mask\neliminates a larger fraction of candidate tokens at each decoding step,\nincreasing the probability that the intersection of grammar and\nsemantic masks yields a narrow or empty candidate set.\nIn the limiting case, the system falls back to grammar-only constraints,\npreserving structural validity but forgoing domain/range enforcement\nfor that token position.\nConversely, SciERC's looser constraints leave more valid alternatives\nfor the model to redirect probability mass toward, enabling\nnear-complete DR elimination.\nThe DR reduction thus scales inversely with constraint tightness:\n45\\% on DocRED (tight) vs.\\ 94\\% on SciERC (loose).\n% DocRED: DR_reduction=45%; SciERC: DR_reduction=94%\nThis represents, to our knowledge, the first empirical characterization\nof how ontology constraint properties modulate decoding-time enforcement\neffectiveness; we develop this analysis further in\nSection~\\ref{sec:discussion}.\n",

phase2_docred_grammar_only: ET=0, RV=0.0003\nHowever, domain/range violations \\emph{increase} from 18.46\\% to\n26.17\\% (+42\\% relative).\n% phase2_docred_grammar_only: DR=0.2617\nThis counter-intuitive result has a structural explanation.\nIn the unconstrained baseline, 29.66\\% of triples contain\nout-of-vocabulary relation names and are classified as RV violations;\nbecause no schema entry exists for an invalid relation name,\nthese triples \\emph{cannot} be evaluated against domain/range rules\nand are therefore absent from the DR count.\nWhen grammar constraints force all relation names into the valid\nvocabulary, these formerly RV-classified triples receive valid\nrelation names---but the associated entity types frequently violate\nthe domain/range rules of the assigned relation, surfacing as new\nDR violations.\nThe grammar layer thus \\emph{reveals} latent type incompatibilities\nrather than creating them, directly motivating the semantic layer:\nwithout domain/range enforcement, grammar alone makes the type\nviolation landscape worse, not better.\n\n% ──────────────────────────────────────────\n%  (c) Enabling dependency\n% ──────────────────────────────────────────\n\\paragraph{Enabling dependency confirmed across datasets.}\nSemantic constraints alone reduce DR by 25\\%\n(18.46\\%\\,$\\to$\\,13.84\\%) but cannot eliminate relation vocabulary\nviolations (RV\\,=\\,29.87\\%) or entity type violations\n(ET\\,=\\,10.44\\%).\n% phase2_docred_semantic_only: DR=0.1384, RV=0.2987, ET=0.1044\nThe elevated ET under semantic-only decoding (10.44\\% vs.\\ 8.18\\%\nunconstrained) reflects a genuine trajectory perturbation:\nconstrained logit masking shifts the generation distribution,\ncausing the model to produce more non-standard entity types\n(e.g., \\textsc{Work}, \\textsc{Event}, \\textsc{Ethnic\\_Group})\nthat fall outside DocRED's canonical six-type vocabulary.\n% Top non-standard types: WORK (823), EVENT (743), ETHNIC_GROUP (137)\nWe discuss this side effect in the limitations.\nCombining both layers (Full S1) reduces DR a further 26\\% relative to\nthe semantic-only configuration (13.84\\%\\,$\\to$\\,10.19\\%),\n% phase2_docred_full_s1: DR=0.1019; delta=(13.84-10.19)/13.84=0.264\nreplicating the enabling dependency observed on SciERC:\nwithout grammar-enforced vocabulary constraints, the semantic layer\nencounters out-of-vocabulary relation types for which no domain/range\nrules exist, limiting its enforcement capacity.\nThe consistency of this pattern across two datasets with substantially\ndifferent ontology sizes (7 vs.\\ 96 relation types) and violation\nprofiles (SciERC RV\\,=\\,1.39\\% vs.\\ DocRED RV\\,=\\,29.66\\%) confirms\nthat the grammar$\\to$semantic enabling dependency is a general\narchitectural property of the framework, not an artifact of a\nparticular schema.\n\n% ──────────────────────────────────────────\n%  (d) Constraint tightness scaling\n% ──────────────────────────────────────────\n\\paragraph{Constraint tightness governs enforcement ceiling.}\nThe residual DR on DocRED (10.19\\%) substantially exceeds that of\nSciERC (0.93\\%, Section~\\ref{sec:scierc-results}).\nThis gap is explained by per-relation \\emph{constraint tightness}:\nDocRED relations admit on average only 5.68 valid entity type pairs\n(mean tightness\\,=\\,0.842, i.e., 84.2\\% of entity type pairs violate\ndomain/range rules), compared to 23 valid pairs on SciERC\n(tightness\\,=\\,0.333).\n% DocRED: avg_valid_pairs=5.68, avg_tightness=0.842\n% SciERC: avg_valid_pairs=23, avg_tightness=0.333\nWith tighter per-relation constraints, the semantic layer's logit mask\neliminates a larger fraction of candidate tokens at each decoding step,\nincreasing the probability that the intersection of grammar and\nsemantic masks yields a narrow or empty candidate set.\nIn the limiting case, the system falls back to grammar-only constraints,\npreserving structural validity but forgoing domain/range enforcement\nfor that token position.\nConversely, SciERC's looser constraints leave more valid alternatives\nfor the model to redirect probability mass toward, enabling\nnear-complete DR elimination.\nThe DR reduction thus scales inversely with constraint tightness:\n45\\% on DocRED (tight) vs.\\ 94\\% on SciERC (loose).\n% DocRED: DR_reduction=45%; SciERC: DR_reduction=94%\nThis represents, to our knowledge, the first empirical characterization\nof how ontology constraint properties modulate decoding-time enforcement\neffectiveness; we develop this analysis further in\nSection~\\ref{sec:discussion}.\n",

Grammar layer\n% ──────────────────────────────────────────\n\\paragraph{Grammar layer: structural guarantees generalize, but surface latent violations.}\nGrammar-only constraints generalize to DocRED's 96-relation ontology,\neliminating entity type violations (2.48\\%\\,$\\to$\\,0\\%) and\nnear-eliminating relation vocabulary violations\n(29.66\\%\\,$\\to$\\,0.03\\%).\n% phase2_docred_grammar_only: ET=0, RV=0.0003\nHowever, domain/range violations \\emph{increase} from 18.46\\% to\n26.17\\% (+42\\% relative).\n% phase2_docred_grammar_only: DR=0.2617\nThis counter-intuitive result has a structural explanation.\nIn the unconstrained baseline, 29.66\\% of triples contain\nout-of-vocabulary relation names and are classified as RV violations;\nbecause no schema entry exists for an invalid relation name,\nthese triples \\emph{cannot} be evaluated against domain/range rules\nand are therefore absent from the DR count.\nWhen grammar constraints force all relation names into the valid\nvocabulary, these formerly RV-classified triples receive valid\nrelation names---but the associated entity types frequently violate\nthe domain/range rules of the assigned relation, surfacing as new\nDR violations.\nThe grammar layer thus \\emph{reveals} latent type incompatibilities\nrather than creating them, directly motivating the semantic layer:\nwithout domain/range enforcement, grammar alone makes the type\nviolation landscape worse, not better.\n\n% ──────────────────────────────────────────\n%  (c) Enabling dependency\n% ──────────────────────────────────────────\n\\paragraph{Enabling dependency confirmed across datasets.}\nSemantic constraints alone reduce DR by 25\\%\n(18.46\\%\\,$\\to$\\,13.84\\%) but cannot eliminate relation vocabulary\nviolations (RV\\,=\\,29.87\\%) or entity type violations\n(ET\\,=\\,10.44\\%).\n% phase2_docred_semantic_only: DR=0.1384, RV=0.2987, ET=0.1044\nThe elevated ET under semantic-only decoding (10.44\\% vs.\\ 8.18\\%\nunconstrained) reflects a genuine trajectory perturbation:\nconstrained logit masking shifts the generation distribution,\ncausing the model to produce more non-standard entity types\n(e.g., \\textsc{Work}, \\textsc{Event}, \\textsc{Ethnic\\_Group})\nthat fall outside DocRED's canonical six-type vocabulary.\n% Top non-standard types: WORK (823), EVENT (743), ETHNIC_GROUP (137)\nWe discuss this side effect in the limitations.\nCombining both layers (Full S1) reduces DR a further 26\\% relative to\nthe semantic-only configuration (13.84\\%\\,$\\to$\\,10.19\\%),\n% phase2_docred_full_s1: DR=0.1019; delta=(13.84-10.19)/13.84=0.264\nreplicating the enabling dependency observed on SciERC:\nwithout grammar-enforced vocabulary constraints, the semantic layer\nencounters out-of-vocabulary relation types for which no domain/range\nrules exist, limiting its enforcement capacity.\nThe consistency of this pattern across two datasets with substantially\ndifferent ontology sizes (7 vs.\\ 96 relation types) and violation\nprofiles (SciERC RV\\,=\\,1.39\\% vs.\\ DocRED RV\\,=\\,29.66\\%) confirms\nthat the grammar$\\to$semantic enabling dependency is a general\narchitectural property of the framework, not an artifact of a\nparticular schema.\n\n% ──────────────────────────────────────────\n%  (d) Constraint tightness scaling\n% ──────────────────────────────────────────\n\\paragraph{Constraint tightness governs enforcement ceiling.}\nThe residual DR on DocRED (10.19\\%) substantially exceeds that of\nSciERC (0.93\\%, Section~\\ref{sec:scierc-results}).\nThis gap is explained by per-relation \\emph{constraint tightness}:\nDocRED relations admit on average only 5.68 valid entity type pairs\n(mean tightness\\,=\\,0.842, i.e., 84.2\\% of entity type pairs violate\ndomain/range rules), compared to 23 valid pairs on SciERC\n(tightness\\,=\\,0.333).\n% DocRED: avg_valid_pairs=5.68, avg_tightness=0.842\n% SciERC: avg_valid_pairs=23, avg_tightness=0.333\nWith tighter per-relation constraints, the semantic layer's logit mask\neliminates a larger fraction of candidate tokens at each decoding step,\nincreasing the probability that the intersection of grammar and\nsemantic masks yields a narrow or empty candidate set.\nIn the limiting case, the system falls back to grammar-only constraints,\npreserving structural validity but forgoing domain/range enforcement\nfor that token position.\nConversely, SciERC's looser constraints leave more valid alternatives\nfor the model to redirect probability mass toward, enabling\nnear-complete DR elimination.\nThe DR reduction thus scales inversely with constraint tightness:\n45\\% on DocRED (tight) vs.\\ 94\\% on SciERC (loose).\n% DocRED: DR_reduction=45%; SciERC: DR_reduction=94%\nThis represents, to our knowledge, the first empirical characterization\nof how ontology constraint properties modulate decoding-time enforcement\neffectiveness; we develop this analysis further in\nSection~\\ref{sec:discussion}.\n",

single-seed\nevaluation and are best characterized as F1-neutral.\nCross-field consistency violations increase modestly from 0.10\\% to\n2.57\\%, substantially lower than the 9.90\\% observed on SciERC;\n% phase2_docred_unconstrained: CF=0.0010; phase2_docred_full_s1: CF=0.0257\nwe discuss this trade-off in Section~\\ref{sec:discussion}.\n\n% ──────────────────────────────────────────\n%  (b) Grammar layer\n% ──────────────────────────────────────────\n\\paragraph{Grammar layer: structural guarantees generalize, but surface latent violations.}\nGrammar-only constraints generalize to DocRED's 96-relation ontology,\neliminating entity type violations (2.48\\%\\,$\\to$\\,0\\%) and\nnear-eliminating relation vocabulary violations\n(29.66\\%\\,$\\to$\\,0.03\\%).\n% phase2_docred_grammar_only: ET=0, RV=0.0003\nHowever, domain/range violations \\emph{increase} from 18.46\\% to\n26.17\\% (+42\\% relative).\n% phase2_docred_grammar_only: DR=0.2617\nThis counter-intuitive result has a structural explanation.\nIn the unconstrained baseline, 29.66\\% of triples contain\nout-of-vocabulary relation names and are classified as RV violations;\nbecause no schema entry exists for an invalid relation name,\nthese triples \\emph{cannot} be evaluated against domain/range rules\nand are therefore absent from the DR count.\nWhen grammar constraints force all relation names into the valid\nvocabulary, these formerly RV-classified triples receive valid\nrelation names---but the associated entity types frequently violate\nthe domain/range rules of the assigned relation, surfacing as new\nDR violations.\nThe grammar layer thus \\emph{reveals} latent type incompatibilities\nrather than creating them, directly motivating the semantic layer:\nwithout domain/range enforcement, grammar alone makes the type\nviolation landscape worse, not better.\n\n% ──────────────────────────────────────────\n%  (c) Enabling dependency\n% ──────────────────────────────────────────\n\\paragraph{Enabling dependency confirmed across datasets.}\nSemantic constraints alone reduce DR by 25\\%\n(18.46\\%\\,$\\to$\\,13.84\\%) but cannot eliminate relation vocabulary\nviolations (RV\\,=\\,29.87\\%) or entity type violations\n(ET\\,=\\,4.22\\%).\n% phase2_docred_semantic_only: DR=0.1384, RV=0.2987, ET=0.0422\nThe elevated ET under semantic-only decoding (10.44\\% vs.\\ 8.18\\%\nunconstrained) reflects a genuine trajectory perturbation:\nconstrained logit masking shifts the generation distribution,\ncausing the model to produce more non-standard entity types\n(e.g., \\textsc{Work}, \\textsc{Event}, \\textsc{Ethnic\\_Group})\nthat fall outside DocRED's canonical six-type vocabulary.\n% Top non-standard types: WORK (823), EVENT (743), ETHNIC_GROUP (137)\nWe discuss this side effect in the limitations.\nCombining both layers (Full S1) reduces DR a further 26\\% relative to\nthe semantic-only configuration (13.84\\%\\,$\\to$\\,10.19\\%),\n% phase2_docred_full_s1: DR=0.1019; delta=(13.84-10.19)/13.84=0.264\nreplicating the enabling dependency observed on SciERC:\nwithout grammar-enforced vocabulary constraints, the semantic layer\nencounters out-of-vocabulary relation types for which no domain/range\nrules exist, limiting its enforcement capacity.\nThe consistency of this pattern across two datasets with substantially\ndifferent ontology sizes (7 vs.\\ 96 relation types) and violation\nprofiles (SciERC RV\\,=\\,1.39\\% vs.\\ DocRED RV\\,=\\,29.66\\%) confirms\nthat the grammar$\\to$semantic enabling dependency is a general\narchitectural property of the framework, not an artifact of a\nparticular schema.\n\n% ──────────────────────────────────────────\n%  (d) Constraint tightness scaling\n% ──────────────────────────────────────────\n\\paragraph{Constraint tightness governs enforcement ceiling.}\nThe residual DR on DocRED (10.19\\%) substantially exceeds that of\nSciERC (0.93\\%, Section~\\ref{sec:scierc-results}).\nThis gap is explained by per-relation \\emph{constraint tightness}:\nDocRED relations admit on average only 5.68 valid entity type pairs\n(mean tightness\\,=\\,0.842, i.e., 84.2\\% of entity type pairs violate\ndomain/range rules), compared to 23 valid pairs on SciERC\n(tightness\\,=\\,0.333).\n% DocRED: avg_valid_pairs=5.68, avg_tightness=0.842\n% SciERC: avg_valid_pairs=23, avg_tightness=0.333\nWith tighter per-relation constraints, the semantic layer's logit mask\neliminates a larger fraction of candidate tokens at each decoding step,\nincreasing the probability that the intersection of grammar and\nsemantic masks yields a narrow or empty candidate set.\nIn the limiting case, the system falls back to grammar-only constraints,\npreserving structural validity but forgoing domain/range enforcement\nfor that token position.\nConversely, SciERC's looser constraints leave more valid alternatives\nfor the model to redirect probability mass toward, enabling\nnear-complete DR elimination.\nThe DR reduction thus scales inversely with constraint tightness:\n45\\% on DocRED (tight) vs.\\ 94\\% on SciERC (loose).\n% DocRED: DR_reduction=45%; SciERC: DR_reduction=94%\nThis represents, to our knowledge, the first empirical characterization\nof how ontology constraint properties modulate decoding-time enforcement\neffectiveness; we develop this analysis further in\nSection~\\ref{sec:discussion}.\n",

relative reduction, while simultaneously\neliminating entity type violations (2.48\\%\\,$\\to$\\,0\\%) and\nnear-eliminating relation vocabulary violations\n(29.66\\%\\,$\\to$\\,0.03\\%).\n% phase2_docred_unconstrained: DR=0.1846, ET=0.0248, RV=0.2966\n% phase2_docred_full_s1: DR=0.1021, ET=0, RV=0.0003\nRelation F1 moves from 7.74 to 8.23 (+0.49\\,pp) and entity F1 from\n58.15 to 59.37 (+1.22\\,pp);\n% phase2_docred_unconstrained: R-F1=0.0774, E-F1=0.5815\n% phase2_docred_full_s1: R-F1=0.0823, E-F1=0.5937\nas with SciERC, these differences are within noise for single-seed\nevaluation and are best characterized as F1-neutral.\nCross-field consistency violations increase modestly from 0.10\\% to\n2.57\\%, substantially lower than the 9.90\\% observed on SciERC;\n% phase2_docred_unconstrained: CF=0.0010; phase2_docred_full_s1: CF=0.0257\nwe discuss this trade-off in Section~\\ref{sec:discussion}.\n\n% ──────────────────────────────────────────\n%  (b) Grammar layer\n% ──────────────────────────────────────────\n\\paragraph{Grammar layer: structural guarantees generalize, but surface latent violations.}\nGrammar-only constraints generalize to DocRED's 96-relation ontology,\neliminating entity type violations (2.48\\%\\,$\\to$\\,0\\%) and\nnear-eliminating relation vocabulary violations\n(29.66\\%\\,$\\to$\\,0.03\\%).\n% phase2_docred_grammar_only: ET=0, RV=0.0003\nHowever, domain/range violations \\emph{increase} from 18.46\\% to\n26.17\\% (+42\\% relative).\n% phase2_docred_grammar_only: DR=0.2617\nThis counter-intuitive result has a structural explanation.\nIn the unconstrained baseline, 29.66\\% of triples contain\nout-of-vocabulary relation names and are classified as RV violations;\nbecause no schema entry exists for an invalid relation name,\nthese triples \\emph{cannot} be evaluated against domain/range rules\nand are therefore absent from the DR count.\nWhen grammar constraints force all relation names into the valid\nvocabulary, these formerly RV-classified triples receive valid\nrelation names---but the associated entity types frequently violate\nthe domain/range rules of the assigned relation, surfacing as new\nDR violations.\nThe grammar layer thus \\emph{reveals} latent type incompatibilities\nrather than creating them, directly motivating the semantic layer:\nwithout domain/range enforcement, grammar alone makes the type\nviolation landscape worse, not better.\n\n% ──────────────────────────────────────────\n%  (c) Enabling dependency\n% ──────────────────────────────────────────\n\\paragraph{Enabling dependency confirmed across datasets.}\nSemantic constraints alone reduce DR by 25\\%\n(18.46\\%\\,$\\to$\\,13.84\\%) but cannot eliminate relation vocabulary\nviolations (RV\\,=\\,29.87\\%) or entity type violations\n(ET\\,=\\,4.22\\%).\n% phase2_docred_semantic_only: DR=0.1384, RV=0.2987, ET=0.0422\nThe elevated ET under semantic-only decoding (4.22\\% vs.\\ 2.48\\%\nunconstrained) reflects a genuine trajectory perturbation:\nconstrained logit masking shifts the generation distribution,\ncausing the model to produce more non-standard entity types\n(e.g., \\textsc{Work}, \\textsc{Event}, \\textsc{Ethnic\\_Group})\nthat fall outside DocRED's canonical six-type vocabulary.\n% Top non-standard types: WORK (823), EVENT (743), ETHNIC_GROUP (137)\nWe discuss this side effect in the limitations.\nCombining both layers (Full S1) reduces DR a further 26\\% relative to\nthe semantic-only configuration (13.84\\%\\,$\\to$\\,10.21\\%),\n% phase2_docred_full_s1: DR=0.1019; delta=(13.84-10.19)/13.84=0.264\nreplicating the enabling dependency observed on SciERC:\nwithout grammar-enforced vocabulary constraints, the semantic layer\nencounters out-of-vocabulary relation types for which no domain/range\nrules exist, limiting its enforcement capacity.\nThe consistency of this pattern across two datasets with substantially\ndifferent ontology sizes (7 vs.\\ 96 relation types) and violation\nprofiles (SciERC RV\\,=\\,1.39\\% vs.\\ DocRED RV\\,=\\,29.66\\%) confirms\nthat the grammar$\\to$semantic enabling dependency is a general\narchitectural property of the framework, not an artifact of a\nparticular schema.\n\n% ──────────────────────────────────────────\n%  (d) Constraint tightness scaling\n% ──────────────────────────────────────────\n\\paragraph{Constraint tightness governs enforcement ceiling.}\nThe residual DR on DocRED (10.19\\%) substantially exceeds that of\nSciERC (0.93\\%, Section~\\ref{sec:scierc-results}).\nThis gap is explained by per-relation \\emph{constraint tightness}:\nDocRED relations admit on average only 5.68 valid entity type pairs\n(mean tightness\\,=\\,0.842, i.e., 84.2\\% of entity type pairs violate\ndomain/range rules), compared to 23 valid pairs on SciERC\n(tightness\\,=\\,0.333).\n% DocRED: avg_valid_pairs=5.68, avg_tightness=0.842\n% SciERC: avg_valid_pairs=23, avg_tightness=0.333\nWith tighter per-relation constraints, the semantic layer's logit mask\neliminates a larger fraction of candidate tokens at each decoding step,\nincreasing the probability that the intersection of grammar and\nsemantic masks yields a narrow or empty candidate set.\nIn the limiting case, the system falls back to grammar-only constraints,\npreserving structural validity but forgoing domain/range enforcement\nfor that token position.\nConversely, SciERC's looser constraints leave more valid alternatives\nfor the model to redirect probability mass toward, enabling\nnear-complete DR elimination.\nThe DR reduction thus scales inversely with constraint tightness:\n45\\% on DocRED (tight) vs.\\ 94\\% on SciERC (loose).\n% DocRED: DR_reduction=45%; SciERC: DR_reduction=94%\nThis represents, to our knowledge, the first empirical characterization\nof how ontology constraint properties modulate decoding-time four-way ablation\n% ──────────────────────────────────────────\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\setlength{\\tabcolsep}{3.6pt}\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}l rrrrr rr@{}}\n\\toprule\n& RV\\,$\\downarrow$ & ET\\,$\\downarrow$ & CF\n  & Rel.\\,$\\uparrow$ & Ent.\\,$\\uparrow$ \\\\\n\\midrule\nUnconstrained\n  & 18.46 & ---\n  & 29.66 & 2.48\n  & 0.10\n  & 7.74  & 58.15 \\\\\nGrammar\n  & 26.17 & $\\uparrow$42\\%\n  & \\textbf{0.03} & \\textbf{0}\n  & 0.07\n  & \\textbf{8.31}  & --- \\\\\nSemantic\n  & 13.84 & $\\downarrow$25\\%\n  & 29.87 & 4.22\n  & 1.05\n  & 7.70  & 57.81 \\\\\nFull (S1)\n  & \\textbf{10.21} & $\\downarrow$\\textbf{45\\%}\n  & \\textbf{0.03} & \\textbf{0}\n  & 2.57\n  & 8.23  & \\textbf{59.37} \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\caption{Four-way ablation on DocRED (998 documents, Qwen3-8B, zero-shot).\nDR: domain/range violation rate;\nRV: relation vocabulary violation rate;\nET: entity type violation rate;\nCF: cross-field consistency violation rate.\n$\\Delta$DR: relative change vs.\\ the unconstrained baseline\n($\\uparrow$: increase; $\\downarrow$: decrease).\nBest per column in \\textbf{bold} (CF excluded---see text).\nF1 scores are multiplied by 100.\nGrammar-only entity F1 is unavailable (---) because the grammar\nconstraint restricts relation vocabulary but does not modify entity\nextraction, preventing meaningful entity-level evaluation in isolation.}\n\\label{tab:docred-ablation}\n\\end{table}\n\n% ──────────────────────────────────────────\n%  (a) Core result\n% ──────────────────────────────────────────\n\\paragraph{Core result: DR halved at zero F1 cost.}\nThe full two-layer configuration reduces domain/range violations from\n18.46\\% to 10.21\\%, a 45\\% relative reduction, while simultaneously\neliminating entity type violations (2.48\\%\\,$\\to$\\,0\\%) and\nnear-eliminating relation vocabulary violations\n(29.66\\%\\,$\\to$\\,0.03\\%).\n% phase2_docred_unconstrained: DR=0.1846, ET=0.0248, RV=0.2966\n% phase2_docred_full_s1: DR=0.1021, ET=0, RV=0.0003\nRelation F1 moves from 7.74 to 8.23 (+0.49\\,pp) and entity F1 from\n58.15 to 59.37 (+1.22\\,pp);\n% phase2_docred_unconstrained: R-F1=0.0774, E-F1=0.5815\n% phase2_docred_full_s1: R-F1=0.0823, E-F1=0.5937\nas with SciERC, these differences are within noise for single-seed\nevaluation and are best characterized as F1-neutral.\nCross-field consistency violations increase modestly from 0.10\\% to\n2.57\\%, substantially lower than the 9.90\\% observed on SciERC;\n% phase2_docred_unconstrained: CF=0.0010; phase2_docred_full_s1: CF=0.0257\nwe discuss this trade-off in Section~\\ref{sec:discussion}.\n\n% ──────────────────────────────────────────\n%  (b) Grammar layer\n% ──────────────────────────────────────────\n\\paragraph{Grammar layer: structural guarantees generalize, but surface latent violations.}\nGrammar-only constraints generalize to DocRED's 96-relation ontology,\neliminating entity type violations (2.48\\%\\,$\\to$\\,0\\%) and\nnear-eliminating relation vocabulary violations\n(29.66\\%\\,$\\to$\\,0.03\\%).\n% phase2_docred_grammar_only: ET=0, RV=0.0003\nHowever, domain/range violations \\emph{increase} from 18.46\\% to\n26.17\\% (+42\\% relative).\n% phase2_docred_grammar_only: DR=0.2617\nThis counter-intuitive result has a structural explanation.\nIn the unconstrained baseline, 29.66\\% of triples contain\nout-of-vocabulary relation names and are classified as RV violations;\nbecause no schema entry exists for an invalid relation name,\nthese triples \\emph{cannot} be evaluated against domain/range rules\nand are therefore absent from the DR count.\nWhen grammar constraints force all relation names into the valid\nvocabulary, these formerly RV-classified triples receive valid\nrelation names---but the associated entity types frequently violate\nthe domain/range rules of the assigned relation, surfacing as new\nDR violations.\nThe grammar layer thus \\emph{reveals} latent type incompatibilities\nrather than creating them, directly motivating the semantic layer:\nwithout domain/range enforcement, grammar alone makes the type\nviolation landscape worse, not better.\n\n% ──────────────────────────────────────────\n%  (c) Enabling dependency\n% ──────────────────────────────────────────\n\\paragraph{Enabling dependency confirmed across datasets.}\nSemantic constraints alone reduce DR by 25\\%\n(18.46\\%\\,$\\to$\\,13.84\\%) but cannot eliminate relation vocabulary\nviolations (RV\\,=\\,29.87\\%) or entity type violations\n(ET\\,=\\,4.22\\%).\n% phase2_docred_semantic_only: DR=0.1384, RV=0.2987, ET=0.0422\nThe elevated ET under semantic-only decoding (4.22\\% vs.\\ 2.48\\%\nunconstrained) reflects a genuine trajectory perturbation:\nconstrained logit masking shifts the generation distribution,\ncausing the model to produce more non-standard entity types\n(e.g., \\textsc{Work}, \\textsc{Event}, \\textsc{Ethnic\\_Group})\nthat fall outside DocRED's canonical six-type vocabulary.\n% Top non-standard types: WORK (823), EVENT (743), ETHNIC_GROUP (137)\nWe discuss this side effect in the limitations.\nCombining both layers (Full S1) reduces DR a further 26\\% relative to\nthe semantic-only configuration (13.84\\%\\,$\\to$\\,10.21\\%),\n% phase2_docred_full_s1: DR=0.1021; delta=(13.84-10.21)/13.84=0.262\nreplicating the enabling dependency observed on SciERC:\nwithout grammar-enforced vocabulary constraints, the semantic layer\nencounters out-of-vocabulary relation types for which no domain/range\nrules exist, limiting its enforcement capacity.\nThe consistency of this pattern across two datasets with substantially\ndifferent ontology sizes (7 vs.\\ 96 relation types) and violation\nprofiles (SciERC RV\\,=\\,1.39\\% vs.\\ DocRED RV\\,=\\,29.66\\%) confirms\nthat the grammar$\\to$semantic enabling dependency is a general\narchitectural property of the framework, not an artifact of a\nparticular schema.\n\n% ──────────────────────────────────────────\n%  (d) Constraint tightness scaling\n% ──────────────────────────────────────────\n\\paragraph{Constraint tightness governs enforcement ceiling.}\nThe residual DR on DocRED (10.21\\%) substantially exceeds that of\nSciERC (0.93\\%, Section~\\ref{sec:scierc-results}).\nThis gap is explained by per-relation \\emph{constraint tightness}:\nDocRED relations admit on average only 5.68 valid entity type pairs\n(mean tightness\\,=\\,0.842, i.e., 84.2\\% of entity type pairs violate\ndomain/range rules), compared to 23 valid pairs on SciERC\n(tightness\\,=\\,0.333).\n% DocRED: avg_valid_pairs=5.68, avg_tightness=0.842\n% SciERC: avg_valid_pairs=23, avg_tightness=0.333\nWith tighter per-relation constraints, the semantic layer's logit mask\neliminates a larger fraction of candidate tokens at each decoding step,\nincreasing the probability that the intersection of grammar and\nsemantic masks yields a narrow or empty candidate set.\nIn the limiting case, the system falls back to grammar-only constraints,\npreserving structural validity but forgoing domain/range enforcement\nfor that token position.\nConversely, SciERC's looser constraints leave more valid alternatives\nfor the model to redirect probability mass toward, enabling\nnear-complete DR elimination.\nThe DR reduction thus scales inversely with constraint tightness:\n45\\% on DocRED (tight) vs.\\ 94\\% on SciERC (loose).\n% DocRED: DR_reduction=45%; SciERC: DR_reduction=94%\nThis represents, to our knowledge, the first empirical characterization\nof how ontology constraint properties modulate decoding-time enforcement\neffectiveness; we develop this analysis further in\nSection~\\ref{sec:discussion}.\n",

normalization.\n\n\\subsection{Cross-Dataset Validation on DocRED}\n\\label{sec:docred-results}\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:docred-ablation} presents the four-way ablation on DocRED,\na document-level relation extraction benchmark with 96 Wikidata relation\ntypes---14$\\times$ the ontology size of SciERC\n(Section~\\ref{sec:scierc-results}).\nAll experiments use Qwen3-8B in a zero-shot setting on 998 test \\multicolumn{5}{c}{\\textbf{Violation Rates (\\%)}}\n& \\multicolumn{2}{c}{\\textbf{F1}} \\\\\n\\cmidrule(lr){2-6} \\cmidrule(l){7-8}\n\\textbf{Mode}\n  & DR\\,$\\downarrow$ & $\\Delta$DR\n  & RV\\,$\\downarrow$ & ET\\,$\\downarrow$ & CF\n  & Rel.\\,$\\uparrow$ & Ent.\\,$\\uparrow$ \\\\\n\\midrule\nUnconstrained\n  & 18.46 & ---\n  & 29.66 & 2.48\n  & 0.10\n  & 7.74  & 58.15 \\\\\nGrammar\n  & 26.17 & $\\uparrow$42\\%\n  & \\textbf{0.03} & \\textbf{0}\n  & 0.07\n  & \\textbf{8.31}  & --- \\\\\nSemantic\n  & 13.84 & $\\downarrow$25\\%\n  & 29.87 & 4.22\n  & 1.05\n  & 7.70  & 57.81 \\\\\nFull (S1)\n  & \\textbf{10.21} & $\\downarrow$\\textbf{45\\%}\n  & \\textbf{0.03} & \\textbf{0}\n  & 2.57\n  & 8.23  & \\textbf{59.37} \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\caption{Four-way ablation on DocRED (998 documents, Qwen3-8B, zero-shot).\nDR: domain/range violation rate;\nRV: relation vocabulary violation rate;\nET: entity type violation rate;\nCF: cross-field consistency violation rate.\n$\\Delta$DR: relative change vs.\\ the unconstrained baseline\n($\\uparrow$: increase; $\\downarrow$: decrease).\nBest per column in \\textbf{bold} (CF excluded---see text).\nF1 scores are multiplied by 100.\nGrammar-only entity F1 is unavailable (---) because the grammar\nconstraint restricts relation vocabulary but does not modify entity\nextraction, preventing meaningful entity-level evaluation in isolation.\nEntity type violation rates reflect per-unique-entity counting (checker v2).\nStatistical significance assessed via bootstrap resampling (10{,}000 iterations).}\n\\label{tab:docred-ablation}\n\\end{table}\n\n% ──────────────────────────────────────────\n%  (a) Core result\n% ──────────────────────────────────────────\n\\paragraph{Core result: DR halved at zero F1 cost.}\nThe full two-layer configuration reduces domain/range violations from\n18.46\\% to 10.21\\%, a 45\\% relative reduction, while simultaneously\neliminating entity type violations (2.48\\%\\,$\\to$\\,0\\%) and\nnear-eliminating relation vocabulary violations\n(29.66\\%\\,$\\to$\\,0.03\\%).\n% phase2_docred_unconstrained: DR=0.1846, ET=0.0248, RV=0.2966\n% phase2_docred_full_s1: DR=0.1021, ET=0, RV=0.0003\nRelation F1 moves from 7.74 to 8.23 ($+$0.49\\,pp; bootstrap 95\\% CI [$+$0.23, $+$0.81]\\,pp, $p < 0.001$, 10{,}000 iterations)\nand entity F1 from 58.15 to 59.37 (+1.22\\,pp).\n% phase2_docred_unconstrained: R-F1=0.0774, E-F1=0.5815\n% phase2_docred_full_s1: R-F1=0.0823, E-F1=0.5937\n% bootstrap: delta=+0.50pp, CI=[+0.23,+0.81], p<0.001\nThe relation F1 improvement is statistically significant, attributable\nto grammar-layer elimination of 5{,}189 out-of-vocabulary relation\npredictions that are counted as false positives under the all-valid-set\nprotocol (Section~\\ref{sec:discussion}).\nCross-field consistency violations increase modestly from 0.10\\% to\n2.57\\%, substantially lower than the 9.90\\% observed on SciERC;\n% phase2_docred_unconstrained: CF=0.0010; phase2_docred_full_s1: CF=0.0257\nwe discuss this trade-off in Section~\\ref{sec:discussion}.\n\n% ──────────────────────────────────────────\n%  (b) Grammar layer\n% ──────────────────────────────────────────\n\\paragraph{Grammar layer: structural guarantees generalize, but surface latent violations.}\nGrammar-only constraints generalize to DocRED's 96-relation ontology,\neliminating entity type violations (2.48\\%\\,$\\to$\\,0\\%) and\nnear-eliminating relation vocabulary violations\n(29.66\\%\\,$\\to$\\,0.03\\%).\n% phase2_docred_grammar_only: ET=0, RV=0.0003\nHowever, domain/range violations \\emph{increase} from 18.46\\% to\n26.17\\% (+42\\% relative).\n% phase2_docred_grammar_only: DR=0.2617\nThis counter-intuitive result has a structural explanation.\nIn the unconstrained baseline, 29.66\\% of triples contain\nout-of-vocabulary relation names and are classified as RV violations;\nbecause no schema entry exists for an invalid relation name,\nthese triples \\emph{cannot} be evaluated against domain/range rules\nand are therefore absent from the DR count.\nWhen grammar constraints force all relation names into the valid\nvocabulary, these formerly RV-classified triples receive valid\nrelation names---but the associated entity types frequently violate\nthe domain/range rules of the assigned relation, surfacing as new\nDR violations.\nThe grammar layer thus \\emph{reveals} latent type incompatibilities\nrather than creating them, directly motivating the semantic layer:\nwithout domain/range enforcement, grammar alone makes the type\nviolation landscape worse, not better.\n\n% ──────────────────────────────────────────\n%  (c) Enabling dependency\n% ──────────────────────────────────────────\n\\paragraph{Enabling dependency confirmed across datasets.}\nSemantic constraints alone reduce DR by 25\\%\n(18.46\\%\\,$\\to$\\,13.84\\%) but cannot eliminate relation vocabulary\nviolations (RV\\,=\\,29.87\\%) or entity type violations\n(ET\\,=\\,4.22\\%).\n% phase2_docred_semantic_only: DR=0.1384, RV=0.2987, ET=0.0422\nThe elevated ET under semantic-only decoding (4.22\\% vs.\\ 2.48\\%\nunconstrained) reflects a genuine trajectory perturbation:\nconstrained logit masking shifts the generation distribution,\ncausing the model to produce more non-standard entity types\n(e.g., \\textsc{Work}, \\textsc{Event}, \\textsc{Ethnic\\_Group})\nthat fall outside DocRED's canonical six-type vocabulary.\n% Top non-standard types: WORK (823), EVENT (743), ETHNIC_GROUP (137)\nWe discuss this side effect in the limitations.\nCombining both layers (Full S1) reduces DR a further 26\\% relative to\nthe semantic-only configuration (13.84\\%\\,$\\to$\\,10.21\\%),\n% phase2_docred_full_s1: DR=0.1021; delta=(13.84-10.21)/13.84=0.262\nreplicating the enabling dependency observed on SciERC:\nwithout grammar-enforced vocabulary constraints, the semantic layer\nencounters out-of-vocabulary relation types for which no domain/range\nrules exist, limiting its enforcement capacity.\nThe consistency of this pattern across two datasets with substantially\ndifferent ontology sizes (7 vs.\\ 96 relation types) and violation\nprofiles (SciERC RV\\,=\\,1.39\\% vs.\\ DocRED RV\\,=\\,29.66\\%) confirms\nthat the grammar$\\to$semantic enabling dependency is a general\narchitectural property of the framework, not an artifact of a\nparticular schema.\n\n% ──────────────────────────────────────────\n%  (d) Constraint tightness scaling\n% ──────────────────────────────────────────\n\\paragraph{Constraint tightness governs enforcement ceiling.}\nThe residual DR on DocRED (10.21\\%) substantially exceeds that of\nSciERC (0.93\\%, Section~\\ref{sec:scierc-results}).\nThis gap is explained by per-relation \\emph{constraint tightness}:\nDocRED relations admit on average only 5.68 valid entity type pairs\n(mean tightness\\,=\\,0.842, i.e., 84.2\\% of entity type pairs violate\ndomain/range rules), compared to 23 valid pairs on SciERC\n(tightness\\,=\\,0.333).\n% DocRED: avg_valid_pairs=5.68, avg_tightness=0.842\n% SciERC: avg_valid_pairs=23, avg_tightness=0.333\nWith tighter per-relation constraints, the semantic layer's logit mask\neliminates a larger fraction of candidate tokens at each decoding step,\nincreasing the probability that the intersection of grammar and\nsemantic masks yields a narrow or empty candidate set.\nIn the limiting case, the system falls back to grammar-only constraints,\npreserving structural validity but forgoing domain/range enforcement\nfor that token position.\nConversely, SciERC's looser constraints leave more valid alternatives\nfor the model to redirect probability mass toward, enabling\nnear-complete DR elimination.\nThe DR reduction thus scales inversely with constraint tightness:\n45\\% on DocRED (tight) vs.\\ 94\\% on SciERC (loose).\n% DocRED: DR_reduction=45%; SciERC: DR_reduction=94%\nThis represents, to our knowledge, the first empirical characterization\nof how ontology constraint properties modulate decoding-time enforcement\neffectiveness; we develop this analysis further in\nSection~\\ref{sec:discussion}.\n",

  "new_string": "Section~\\ref{sec:discussion}.\n\n% ──────────────────────────────────────────\n%  (d) Comparison with post-hoc filtering\n% ──────────────────────────────────────────\n\\paragraph{Comparison with post-hoc filtering.}\nAn alternative to decoding-time constraint enforcement is post-hoc filtering:\ngenerating triples without constraints and discarding those that violate\nschema rules afterward.\n% posthoc_report_docred: filtering applied to phase2_docred_unconstrained\nOn DocRED, post-hoc filtering removes 8{,}419 of 17{,}497 triples\n(48.1\\% rejection rate), including 5{,}189 out-of-vocabulary predictions,\n692 entity type violations, and 2{,}538 domain/range violations.\n% posthoc_docred: removed_rv=5189, removed_et=692, removed_dr=2538\nThis raises precision from 6.55\\% to 11.22\\% but reduces recall from\n9.46\\% to 8.40\\%, as 127 gold-standard triples are discarded along with\nthe violations (relation F1: 7.74\\,$\\to$\\,9.61).\n% posthoc_docred: P_before=0.0655, P_after=0.1122, R_before=0.0946, R_after=0.0840\n% posthoc_docred: F1_before=0.0774, F1_after=0.0961, tp_before=1137, tp_after=1010\nOn SciERC, the pattern is analogous: 16.8\\% of triples are rejected, recall\ndrops from 11.40\\% to 10.68\\%, and 7 gold-standard triples are lost.\n% posthoc_scierc: rejection_rate=0.1676, R_before=0.114, R_after=0.1068\n% posthoc_scierc: tp_before=111, tp_after=104\nOntoCoDec avoids this coverage--precision trade-off by guiding generation\ntoward schema-compliant triples rather than discarding non-compliant ones,\nand operates as a single decoding pass without a separate filtering stage.\n\n% ──────────────────────────────────────────\n%  (e) Constraint tightness scaling\n% ──────────────────────────────────────────"
}

SciERC}\n\\label{sec:scierc-results}\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:scierc-ablation} presents the five-way ablation on SciERC,\nevaluating each constraint layer independently and in combination.\nAll experiments use Qwen3-8B in a zero-shot setting on 551 test documents\nwith identical prompts and decoding parameters;\nonly the active constraint layers differ.\n\n% ──────────────────────────────────────────\n%  Table: SciERC five-way ablation\n% ──────────────────────────────────────────\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\setlength{\\tabcolsep}{3.6pt}\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}l rrrrr rr@{}}\n\\toprule\n& \\multicolumn{5}{c}{\\textbf{Violation Rates (\\%)}}\n& \\multicolumn{2}{c}{\\textbf{F1}} \\\\\n\\cmidrule(lr){2-6} \\cmidrule(l){7-8}\n\\textbf{Mode}\n  & DR\\,$\\downarrow$ & $\\Delta$DR\n  & RV\\,$\\downarrow$ & ET\\,$\\downarrow$ & CF\n  & Rel.\\,$\\uparrow$ & Ent.\\,$\\uparrow$ \\\\\n\\midrule\nUnconstrained\n  & 15.37 & ---\n  & 1.39  & 0.40\n  & 0.08\n  & 8.23  & 31.01 \\\\\nGrammar\n  & 14.84 & $\\downarrow$3\\%\n  & \\textbf{0} & \\textbf{0}\n  & 0.08\n  & 8.23  & 30.98 \\\\\nSemantic\n  & 11.65 & $\\downarrow$24\\%\n  & 1.51  & 0.40\n  & 2.71\n  & 8.15  & 31.01 \\\\\nFull (S1)\n  & \\textbf{0.93} & $\\downarrow$\\textbf{94\\%}\n  & \\textbf{0} & \\textbf{0}\n  & 9.90\n  & \\textbf{8.36} & 30.98 \\\\\nFull (S2)\n  & 0.97  & $\\downarrow$94\\%\n  & \\textbf{0} & \\textbf{0}\n  & 8.03\n  & 8.03  & \\textbf{31.90} \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\caption{Five-way ablation on SciERC (551 documents, Qwen3-8B, zero-shot).\nDR: domain/range violation rate;\nRV: relation vocabulary violation rate;\nET: entity type violation rate;\nCF: cross-field consistency violation rate.\n$\\Delta$DR: relative DR reduction vs.\\ the unconstrained baseline.\n$\\downarrow$\\,/\\,$\\uparrow$: lower\\,/\\,higher is better.\nBest per column in \\textbf{bold} (CF excluded---see text).\nF1 scores are multiplied by 100.}\n\\label{tab:scierc-ablation}\n\\end{table}\n\n% ──────────────────────────────────────────\n%  (a) Core result\n% ──────────────────────────────────────────\n\\paragraph{Core result: near-complete DR elimination at zero F1 cost.}\nThe full two-layer constraint configuration (S1) reduces domain/range\nviolations from 15.37\\% to 0.93\\%, a 94\\% relative reduction,\n% phase2_scierc_unconstrained: DR=0.1537; phase2_scierc_full_s1: DR=0.0093\nwhile relation F1 moves from 8.23 to 8.36 (+0.13\\,pp).\n% phase2_scierc_unconstrained: R-F1=0.0823; phase2_scierc_full_s1: R-F1=0.0836\nThis difference is not statistically significant and is best\ncharacterized as F1-neutral: constraint enforcement neither improves\nnor degrades extraction quality.\nAt the per-relation level, violations drop from 42.42\\% to 2.82\\% on\n\\textsc{Compare}, from 33.54\\% to 1.21\\% on \\textsc{Feature-of}, and\nreach 0\\% on four of seven relation types\n(\\textsc{Conjunction}, \\textsc{Hyponym-of}, \\textsc{Part-of},\n\\textsc{Used-for}).\n% phase2_scierc_full_s1 per_relation_dr: Compare=0.0282,\n%   Feature-of=0.0121, Hyponym-of=0, Part-of=0, Used-for=0, Conjunction=0\nHowever, full constraint enforcement elevates cross-field consistency\nviolations from 0.08\\% to 9.90\\%;\n% phase2_scierc_unconstrained: CF=0.0008; phase2_scierc_full_s1: CF=0.099\nwe analyze this trade-off in Section~\\ref{sec:discussion}.\n\n% ──────────────────────────────────────────\n%  (b) Grammar layer\n% ──────────────────────────────────────────\n\\paragraph{Grammar layer: cost-free structural guarantees.}\nGrammar-only constraints completely eliminate both relation vocabulary\nviolations (1.39\\%\\,$\\to$\\,0\\%) and entity type violations\n(0.40\\%\\,$\\to$\\,0\\%), with no measurable impact on F1\n(R-F1 8.23, E-F1 30.98).\n% phase2_scierc_grammar_only: RV=0, ET=0, R-F1=0.0823, E-F1=0.3098\nDomain/range violations remain effectively unchanged\n(15.37\\%\\,$\\to$\\,14.84\\%, $-$0.53\\,pp), confirming that grammar\nconstraints enforce output structure---valid JSON, correct relation\nvocabulary, valid entity types---but do not address cross-field\nsemantic constraints.\n% phase2_scierc_grammar_only: DR=0.1484\nThe grammar layer thus provides two categories of violation elimination\nat zero cost, establishing the structural preconditions on which the\nsemantic layer depends.\n\n% ──────────────────────────────────────────\n%  (c) Enabling dependency\n% ──────────────────────────────────────────\n\\paragraph{Enabling dependency: grammar enables semantic enforcement.}\nApplying semantic constraints alone reduces DR by only 24\\%\n(15.37\\%\\,$\\to$\\,11.65\\%) and, critically, cannot eliminate\nrelation vocabulary violations (RV\\,=\\,1.51\\%) or entity type\nviolations (ET\\,=\\,0.40\\%).\n% phase2_scierc_semantic_only: DR=0.1165, RV=0.0151, ET=0.004\nWhen the grammar layer is added (Full S1), DR drops a further 92\\%\nrelative to the semantic-only configuration\n(11.65\\%\\,$\\to$\\,0.93\\%).\n% phase2_scierc_full_s1: DR=0.0093; delta=(11.65-0.93)/11.65=0.920\nThis demonstrates an \\emph{enabling dependency} rather than mere\nadditivity: without grammar-enforced vocabulary constraints, the\nsemantic layer encounters out-of-vocabulary relation types for which\nno domain/range rules exist, rendering its type checking ineffective.\nThe two layers are therefore complementary with a prerequisite\nstructure---grammar must be active for semantic constraints to reach\ntheir full enforcement capacity.\n\n% ──────────────────────────────────────────\n%  (d) S1 vs S2\n% ──────────────────────────────────────────\n\\paragraph{S1 vs.\\ S2: single-pass suffices.}\nThe two-pass strategy (S2) achieves comparable DR\n(0.97\\% vs.\\ 0.93\\%) but lower relation F1\n(8.03 vs.\\ 8.36, $-$0.33\\,pp) and generates fewer relation triples\n(1{,}644 vs.\\ 1{,}729).\n% phase2_scierc_full_s2: DR=0.0097, R-F1=0.0803, triples=1644\n% phase2_scierc_full_s1: DR=0.0093, R-F1=0.0836, triples=1729\nS2 shows a modestly lower CF rate (8.03\\% vs.\\ 9.90\\%), but both\nconfigurations exhibit substantially elevated CF relative to the\nunconstrained baseline (0.08\\%), indicating that two-pass decoding\ndoes not resolve the cross-field consistency trade-off\n(Section~\\ref{sec:discussion}).\n% phase2_scierc_full_s2: CF=0.0803; phase2_scierc_full_s1: CF=0.099\nThe two-pass separation offers no clear advantage over single-pass\nenforcement; we adopt S1 as the primary configuration for its\nsimplicity and marginally stronger extraction performance.\n",

106	\paragraph{Grammar layer: structural guarantees generalize, but surface latent violations.}
107	Grammar-only constraints generalize to DocRED's 96-relation ontology,
108	eliminating entity type violations (2.48\%\,$\to$\,0\%) and
109	near-eliminating relation vocabulary violations
110	(29.66\%\,$\to$\,0.03\%).
111	% phase2_docred_grammar_only: ET=0, RV=0.0003

113	26.17\% (+42\% relative).
114	% phase2_docred_grammar_only: DR=0.2617
115	This counter-intuitive result has a structural explanation.
116	In the unconstrained baseline, 29.66\% of triples contain
117	out-of-vocabulary relation names and are classified as RV violations;
118	because no schema entry exists for an invalid relation name,
119	these triples \emph{cannot} be evaluated against domain/range rules
120	and are therefore absent from the DR count.
121	When grammar constraints force all relation names into the valid
122	vocabulary, these formerly RV-classified triples receive valid
123	relation names---but the associated entity types frequently violate
124	the domain/range rules of the assigned relation, surfacing as new
125	DR violations.
126	The grammar layer thus \emph{reveals} latent type incompatibilities
127	rather than creating them, directly motivating the semantic layer:
128	without domain/range enforcement, grammar alone makes the type
129	violation landscape worse, not better.
130	
131	% ──────────────────────────────────────────
132	%  (c) Enabling dependency

134	\paragraph{Enabling dependency confirmed across datasets.}
135	Semantic constraints alone reduce DR by 25\%

137	violations (RV\,=\,29.87\%) or entity type violations
138	(ET\,=\,4.22\%).

141	unconstrained) reflects a genuine trajectory perturbation:
142	constrained logit masking shifts the generation distribution,
143	causing the model to produce more non-standard entity types
144	(e.g., \textsc{Work}, \textsc{Event}, \textsc{Ethnic\_Group})
145	that fall outside DocRED's canonical six-type vocabulary.

150	% phase2_docred_full_s1: DR=0.1021; delta=(13.84-10.21)/13.84=0.262
151	replicating the enabling dependency observed on SciERC:
152	without grammar-enforced vocabulary constraints, the semantic layer
153	encounters out-of-vocabulary relation types for which no domain/range
